Malignant ascites develops when malignant disease of the peritoneum, typically from an underlying gastrointestinal or gynecologic cancer, causes excess fluid to accumulate in the abdominal cavity. The development of malignant ascites portends a poor prognosis and is associated with debilitating symptoms and a reduced quality of life. While several palliative therapies exist for the management of these symptoms, none have proven curative or have demonstrated a prolonged therapeutic benefit. Thus, various experimental treatments are currently under investigation. These include immunotherapies and targeted therapies, monoclonal antibodies, herbal derivatives, viral therapies, devices, and more. This review describes these experimental therapies and their efficacy in managing malignant ascites. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access no pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript yes pmc-prop-open-manuscript yes pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes 1. Introduction Malignant ascites (MA) arises in the setting of malignant peritoneal disease as a manifestation of underlying gastrointestinal or gynecological cancer metastases. Peritoneal metastases are typically associated with a worse survival when compared to other metastatic sites. Furthermore, MA is an indicator of advanced disease associated with debilitating symptomatology including abdominal discomfort, nausea, vomiting, and anorexia. 1
[w5fb5ac-2] The management of MA is challenging, particularly in cases in which surgical treatment of the underlying cancer is not feasible. In these cases, the aim of regional or surgical management is palliation. While many palliative approaches are described in the literature, the effectiveness of these treatments is often limited and temporary. In recent years, there has been growing interest in novel therapeutic approaches for the management of MA, including targeted therapies, immunotherapies, herbal derivatives, and devices. The purpose of this review is to provide a contemporary representation of these emerging therapies.
[w5fb5ac-3] 2. Current Standard Therapies Today, there are five primary treatments used in the management of MA: paracentesis, diuretics, peritoneovenous shunting, permanent catheters, and intraperitoneal (i.p.) chemotherapy. Clinician preference on their use varies widely. 2 The most commonly used treatment is serial paracenteses, which can drain several liters of i.p. fluid and palliate common symptoms in approximately 90% of patients. 2 , 3 Unfortunately, symptoms often returns within several weeks as the ascitic fluid reaccumulates, and serial paracenteses carry increasing risk of infection, bowel perforation, peritonitis, hypotension, and protein and electrolyte disturbances. 4
[w5fb5ac-4] For those requiring serial paracenteses and who have a life expectancy of greater than 2–3 months, permanent catheters are often used. 5 The advantages include increased patient autonomy due to self-drainage, increased convenience due to fewer hospital visits, and decreased discomfort due to fewer procedures. 6 MA control can be achieved in 83% to 100% of patients with a mean patency duration exceeding 50 days, and some case reports have shown effective functionality for well over one year. 4 , 7 However, it is limited by common complications such as peritonitis, leakage, infections, catheter occlusion, and hypotension. 8
[w5fb5ac-5] Standard medical management involves diuretic therapy, with nearly two-thirds of physicians prescribing diuretics for MA. 2 However, due to variability in regimens and few trials assessing their efficacy, there is no consensus on their role in MA, and fewer than half of prescribing physicians consider them effective. A prior review of several cohort studies and case reports, however, suggests that 40–45% of patients may derive some benefit. 1 Importantly, serum ascites albumin gradient (SAAG) > 1.1 g/dL and MA from hepatic metastases portend a more favorable response. 9 It appears that the efficacy of this therapy is largely dependent upon the biology underlying the development of MA.
[w5fb5ac-6] Peritoneovenous shunting uses a one-way valve to return ascitic fluid to the venous system. 10 It allows for fewer paracenteses and preserves serum albumin levels, but has an inferior control rate compared to paracentesis and a mean patency of as little as 9 weeks. 1 It also requires perioperative hospitalization, is associated with several risks including shunt occlusion, ascitic leak, coagulopathy, infection, bleeding, and even death, and is contraindicated in several conditions such as heart failure and renal failure. 1 , 4 , 11 For these reasons, it is typically reserved for patients who have failed other modalities.
[w5fb5ac-7] Finally, i.p. chemotherapy, typically used in conjunction with cytoreductive surgery (CRS) to treat peritoneal carcinomatosis, has shown some benefit in MA even when an optimal cytoreduction is not possible. The hypothesized mechanism is the targeting of microscopic tumor cells on the peritoneal surface from which MA is produced and the inducement of a fibrotic reaction that prevents the reformation of fluid. Pharmacokinetic evidence also suggests that compared to systemic chemotherapy, i.p. treatments improve drug concentrations within the peritoneum, allow for prolonged therapeutic effects, limit systemic toxicities, and more directly target malignant cells. 12 However, these effects depend upon the specific chemotherapy regimen chosen. While some, such as mitoxantrone and mustard agents, have fallen out of favor, other agents such as bleomycin, taxanes, and platinum-based agents have been shown to improve symptoms in up to two-thirds of patients. 1 , 13 – 15 Hyperthermic intraperitoneal chemotherapy (HIPEC) has been widely adopted due to evidence that superheating the chemotherapy to at least 40 degrees Celsius increases cytotoxicity to improve tissue penetration and selectively targets malignant cells within the peritoneal cavity. 16
[w5fb5ac-8] 3. Novel Therapies While these therapies provide palliative benefits in the management of MA, each is limited by side effects and lack of a prolonged therapeutic benefit. As a result, various experimental therapies are under investigation in the hopes of finding a novel solution to this condition. 3.1 Immunotherapy and Targeted Therapy Immunotherapies and targeted therapies have gained interest in the past decade in the management of both solid tumors and MA. 17 While most studies on the subject are small and inconclusive, several therapies have shown preliminary efficacy.
[w5fb5ac-9] 3.1.1 VEGF Inhibitors It is thought that one of the causes of MA is the obstruction of draining lymphatics by tumor cells. 18 In the 1980’s investigators speculated that the underlying culprit in this phenomenon is the secretion of vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) by tumor cells, given that VEGF is more concentrated in MA compared to non-malignant ascites and more prevalent in metastatic as compared to localized tumors. 19 , 20 As such, VEGF inhibition became a target in the treatment of MA ( Table 1a ).
[w5fb5ac-10] Early case series showed that the VEGF inhibitor bevacizumab, administered both i.p. and orally, produced symptomatic relief and a reduction in the ascites reaccumulation rate in several women with refractory MA from ovarian cancer. 21 – 26 Two clinical trials were subsequently initiated. In 2012, researchers randomized 55 patients with recurrent MA from ovarian cancer to receive either placebo or i.v. aflibercept, a recombination human protein and a VEGF inhibitor, every 2 weeks. 27 Although the aflibercept group experienced better mean time to paracentesis (TTP) (55.1 days versus 23.3 days) and paracentesis-free survival (PFS) (42.0 days versus 18.0 days), there was no difference in overall survival (OS). Furthermore, 3 patients in the aflibercept group developed intestinal perforations that led to death, as compared to just 1 patient in the placebo group that developed an intestinal fistula that led to fatal sepsis. Gastrointestinal complications such as perforation are well-known side effects of VEGF inhibitors and their use in this patient population should be undertaken with caution. 28 A smaller single-arm study investigating i.v. aflibercept in a similar patient population found that 10 of 16 patients achieved a 2-fold increase in TTP. 29 Unfortunately, one patient suffered an intestinal perforation.
[w5fb5ac-11] Randomized trials have also been conducted with VEGF inhibitors administered intraperitoneally. In 2016, researchers randomized 53 patients with advanced gastrointestinal cancers and MA to i.p. bevacizumab or placebo. 30 Median PFS was greater in the bevacizumab group compared to the placebo group, though to a non-statistically significant degree. There were no major gastrointestinal events. In 2021 a phase II trial found that in 24 women with MA from ovarian cancer, treatment with i.p. bevacizumab resulted in a 4.3-fold increase in median PFS, though 1 patient developed a perforation and 3 developed a bowel obstruction. 31 A dose-escalation study of i.p. bevacizumab in 13 patients with refractory MA found a disease control rate of 61.5%, with a grade 3 bowel obstruction serving as the dose-limiting toxicity. 32
[w5fb5ac-12] Although VEGF inhibitors carry safety concerns around gastrointestinal perforation and obstruction, these studies demonstrate promising improvements in refractory MA that should be weighed against these risks. 3.1.2 Interferons Interferons (IFN) alpha and gamma are signaling cytokines used to treat a variety of infectious, autoimmune, and inflammatory conditions. They exert their effects through both direct cytotoxicity and immune cell stimulation. Interferons have been evaluated in the management of various cancers, though their use specifically in MA remains limited with few full-text studies available for review ( Table 1b ).
[w5fb5ac-13] The study of IFNs to treat MA arose in the 1980’s when clinicians incidentally found improvement of ascites in patients with refractory ovarian cancer who were administered IFNs as salvage therapy. For example, researchers administered human leukocyte IFN intramuscularly to 5 patients with advanced ovarian carcinoma and found that both patients with pre-treatment MA showed a significant reduction in their ascites production. 33 Similarly, i.p. cisplatin and IFN alpha administered to 24 patients with advanced ovarian cancer resulted in resolution of ascites for 3–7 months in the 3 patients with pre-treatment ascites. 34
[w5fb5ac-14] Following these trials, IFNs were studied as a stand-alone treatment in MA, with mixed results. Ten patients with MA from ovarian cancer were treated with i.p. IFN alpha-2b every 2 weeks for 1–4 treatments, but just half reported symptomatic improvement in abdominal distension and girth. 35 Five of 13 (38%) patients with MA from ovarian cancer responded to treatment with i.p. IFN alpha-2b, though all 5 responders had MA only without masses. 36 Similarly, there was a 40% response rate in 14 patients with MA refractory to chemotherapy treated with i.p. IFN beta, though it’s unclear how the authors defined response rate. 37 Finally, a pilot study of 41 patients with MA again showed moderately positive results. 38 After six 6-hour infusions of i.p. IFN alpha-2b, 12 patients had a complete response, 15 had a partial response, and 14 had no response; the mean time to fluid reaccumulation increased from 11.4 days pre-treatment to 70.5 days post-treatment.
[w5fb5ac-15] Given these mixed results, the use of IFNs in the management of MA remains experimental with significant effort needed to identify appropriate dosages and treatment regimens.
[w5fb5ac-16] 3.1.3 Tumor Necrosis Factor Tumor necrosis factor (TNF) is a pro-inflammatory cytokine implicated in the formation of MA as well as being leveraged as a therapeutic, a dual effect first demonstrated in mouse models of MA from ovarian adenocarcinoma. 39 After early pharmacokinetic trials of TNF in patients with solid tumors demonstrated feasibility and safety, a trial of its use in MA was conducted in 1990. 40 In this study, 23 patients with refractory MA from ovarian cancer were treated with i.p. human recombinant TNF ( Table 1c ). 41 While 20 patients experienced a reduction in ascites for at least 4 weeks, the full text is not available and further details are lacking. A phase I trial published in 1991 produced similarly positive results. 42 In 29 patients with refractory MA from various primary cancers, i.p. TNF-alpha once weekly for between 0–2 months produced a complete response in 16 patients and a partial response in 6 patients.
[w5fb5ac-17] However, a trial in 39 patients with symptomatic MA from ovarian carcinoma or primary peritoneal carcinoma randomized to either paracentesis or paracentesis plus i.p. TNF-alpha was discouraging. 43 No patients in either arm had a complete or partial response, and adverse events were significantly more common in the treatment group. Similarly, 302 patients with MA treated with i.p. TNF produced an objective response rate of just 46%, though the full text is not available. 44
[w5fb5ac-18] These inconsistent results, along with the lack of full-text studies in English, present a challenge for future study, and the possibility that certain dosages of TNF may promote tumor formation makes future investigations especially difficult. 45 A single-center exploratory trial is currently underway in China investigating HIPEC with Cisplatin and i.p. recombinant human TNF in the management of MA from gynecologic cancers. 46
[w5fb5ac-19] 3.1.4 OK-432 OK-432 (Picibanil) is a freeze-dried powder developed through heating Streptococcal Pyogenes in the presence of Penicillin to eliminate the bacteria’s toxin production. 47 In the 1970’s and 1980’s, OK-432 was found to have sclerosing and anti-neoplastic activity through stimulation of natural killer cells, macrophages, neutrophils, and IFNs. 48 Given this purported anti-neoplastic, sclerosing, and immuno-stimulatory activity, in the 1980’s it gained interest in the possible palliation of MA, particularly in Eastern countries ( Table 1d ).
[w5fb5ac-20] A 1983 abstract describes 134 patients with MA from various gastrointestinal primary tumors who were administered i.p. OK-432. 49 Ascites resolved in 76 patients and was reduced in 8 others. The treatment was more effective in patients with positive cytology, with 81.7% of these patients showing a response to therapy compared to just 24.1% of patients with negative cytology. The authors conducted a smaller study of i.p. OK-432 in 12 patients with MA from gastric cancer and found that all patients had resolution of their ascites, though likewise the full text is unavailable. 50
[w5fb5ac-21] Toge et al. showed similarly promising results in their study of i.p. OK-432, administered either continuously or intermittently, in 25 patients with MA from various primary tumors. 51 In the continuous administration group, cytology changed from positive to negative in 100% of patients; in the intermittent group, 100% of patients who received 5 or more doses saw a change in cytology, compared to just 25% of those who received fewer than 5 doses.
[w5fb5ac-22] Othe investigators studied different regimens of OK-432. I.p. OK-432 with Interleukin-2 was administered in patients with MA from gastric or colorectal cancer in two separate studies. 52 , 53 The response rate ranged from 81% to 100% with Interleukin-2 seemingly conferring a synergistic benefit. Other researchers randomized 41 patients with malignant effusions (31 with MA, 8 with malignant pleural effusion, and 2 with both) to receive 1 of 3 intracavitary regimens: chemotherapy alone, OK-432 alone, or combination immunochemotherapy. 54 Compared to the chemotherapy alone group, patients who received OK-432 alone or combination immunochemotherapy had greater reductions in effusion size and greater disappearance of tumor cells within MA. Furthermore, the 1-year survival rate was 0%, 0%, and 17% in the chemotherapy, immunotherapy, and combination groups, respectively. The authors concluded that intracavitary OK-432 was superior to intracavitary chemotherapy in the management of malignant effusions, but that the combination was most beneficial. The largest study to date was conducted in 1998 by reviewing 400 cases of MA from gastrointestinal malignancies treated with i.p. OK-432 over 20 years. 55 The researchers found a response rate of 60% and a reduction of tumor volume in 20% of cases, although the full text is unavailable for review.
[w5fb5ac-23] Given this favorable response rate across hundreds of patients, the synergistic benefits when used alongside other treatments, and the suggestion that it may even improve survival in some patients, OK-432 offers a promising potential therapy that warrants investigation in the United States. 56
[w5fb5ac-24] 3.1.5 Autologous Dendritic and Natural Killer Cell Immunotherapy Autologous dendritic cell immunotherapy is an investigational immunotherapy developed by extracting cells from a patient, processing and expanding them ex vivo, and then reintroducing them. 57 Though a relatively nascent treatment modality that has mostly been studied as an anti-cancer therapy, recent case reports and studies have described its use in MA ( Table 1e ).
[w5fb5ac-25] A 2008 case report describes a 55-year-old man whose MA secondary to metastatic lung cancer was significantly improved with cell-free and concentrated ascites re-infusion therapy alongside i.p. injections of autologous natural killer and dendritic cells taken from his tumor-draining lymph nodes. 58 A 2014 abstract describes a response rate of 40.9% and disease control rate of 77.3% in 22 patients with MA who were treated with i.p. dendritic cell vaccines with cytokine-induced killer cells. 59 Similarly, in 21 patients with MA from various cancers who underwent autologous immunotherapy with the extraction, expansion, and i.p. reinfusion of dendritic cells and natural killer cells, 8 achieved a complete or partial response, 4 experienced stable disease, and 9 developed progressive disease. 60 Some researchers have used other types of immune cells in this treatment modality, and Vc9Vd2 T cells, a specific form of T cell known to play a role in tumor and microbial defense, showed some preliminary efficacy in a small group of patients with MA from gastric cancer. 61
[w5fb5ac-26] Though autologous i.p. immunotherapy remains exploratory, it is likely to garner interest in the future given the increasing role of immunotherapy in cancer care. Further studies of its use in MA must be conducted before recommending its routine use.
[w5fb5ac-27] 3.2 Monoclonal Antibodies Since the first production of a monoclonal antibody in 1975, these man-made proteins have gained attention for their promise to treat a variety of diseases. The role of monoclonal antibodies in MA stems from studies conducted in the 1980’s showing that ovarian cancer cells could be detected through labeling monoclonal antibodies with radioisotopes and performing immunoscintigraphy. 62 While this ultimately never reached the specificity required for these antibodies to become curative cancer treatments, their i.p. administration did localize to cancer cells within MA fairly well. 63 On the basis of these findings, investigations into their role in MA were initiated ( Table 2 ).
[w5fb5ac-28] 3.2.1 AUA1, HMFG1, HMFG2, and H17E2 The first study to assess monoclonal antibodies in MA was a case series published in 1987, in which 24 patients with MA from ovarian cancer were treated with different i.p. injections of 131 I-labeled monoclonal antibodies commonly expressed in ovarian, breast, and gastrointestinal adenocarcinomas: HMFG1, HMFG2, AUA1, and H17E2. 64 Of the 5 patients with MA upon presentation, just 1 demonstrated control of MA for 6 months, though the authors noted that all 5 patients had large-volume disease.
[w5fb5ac-29] In 1988 researchers studied the use of i.p. AUA1 and HMFG2 in two groups: patients with refractory ovarian cancer without ascites and patients with MA refractory to paracentesis and diuretics. 65 None of the 7 patients with ovarian carcinoma saw meaningful tumor regression after treatment with 131 I-conjugated HMFG2. However, in the 5 patients with recurrent MA treated with either the 131 I-conjugated HMFG2 or AUA1 antibody, 3 experienced ascites resolution for a mean of 4 months, 1 died early but had seen disappearance of tumor cells in his ascitic fluid, and 1 experienced progression but had a subpopulation of tumor cells that were antigen-negative. Though these results were not conclusive, they nonetheless demonstrated an early proof-of-concept.
[w5fb5ac-30] 3.2.3 Catumaxomab Trifunctional antibodies have binding sites for 3 different antigens or receptors: CD3 on T-cells, a specific tumor antigen, and the Fc-y receptor on accessory immune cells. 66 This trifunctionality allows these antibodies to exert their anti-neoplastic effects through several pathways, potentially making them superior to bispecific antibodies.
[w5fb5ac-31] In early preclinical studies, Catumaxomab was shown to be particularly promising. A mouse/rat monoclonal antibody, it binds to CD3 on T-cells, the Fc-y receptor on accessory immune cells, and the EpCAM antigen, which is found in 70% to 100% of the tumor cells in malignant effusions and is considered a tumor-specific antigen in MA. 67 Clinical trials of Catumaxomab in MA were initiated in the early 2000’s, and in 2009 it was approved for use in the European Union.
[w5fb5ac-32] In 2005 investigators studied both Catumaxomab and Rexomun, a trifunctional antibody that targets Her2/neu, in 8 patients with symptomatic MA from peritoneal carcinomatosis. 68 Four received Catumaxomab, 1 received Rexomun, and 3 received both; all trifunctional antibodies were administered i.p. over 6–8 hours. All patients experienced relief of symptoms and a reduction in ascites, which correlated with significant drops in the proportion of EpCAM- and HER2/neu-positive tumor cells in the MA. Nearly all patients experienced flu-like symptoms, though none were forced to discontinue treatment.
[w5fb5ac-33] These investigators then conducted a trial of paracentesis with or without Catumaxomab in 258 patients with MA, stratified by ovarian versus non-ovarian origin. 69 Patients underwent four 6-hour i.p. Catutaxomab infusions. In both the ovarian and non-ovarian groups, the Catumaxomab group had a better PFS (46 days versus 11 days) and median TTP (77 days vs 13 days). Furthermore, OS was significantly prolonged in the subset of patients with gastric cancer. Nearly 90% of patients received all 4 doses and side effects were tolerable. Similarly, in 23 patients with MA from ovarian cancer, four 6-hour infusions of i.p. Catumaxomab produced a 99.9% mean reduction in the number of EpCAM-positive tumor cells within ascites and resulted in 22 of 23 patients not requiring paracentesis between the first and last days of the study (a total of 37 days). 70 A 2007 abstract
[w5fb5ac-34] Further trials investigated whether Catumaxomab could be administered as a 3-hour infusion as opposed to a 6-hour infusion, which would allow it to be given on an outpatient basis. While these studies showed positive results, the prevalence of adverse events, including nausea, vomiting, pyrexia, and abdominal pain, limited its feasibility as an outpatient treatment. 71 , 72 In one trial, 40% of patients had to postpone or discontinue treatments due to adverse events despite improvements in TTP and PFS, and the CASIMAS trial showed that nearly half of patients randomized to 3-hour i.p. infusions of Catumaxomab with or without prednisone experienced grade 3 or greater adverse events, with prednisone conferring little benefit on the side effect profile. 73 , 74 Furthermore, it was found that i.p. Catumaxomab may be less feasible in patients undergoing surgery for their disease. For example, researchers treated 41 patients with ovarian cancer undergoing CRS and systemic chemotherapy for their disease with i.p. Catumaxomab administered as an intraoperative bolus followed by 3-hour infusions given on postoperative days 7, 10, 13, and 16. 75 Just 61% of patients completed all 5 postoperative infusions, 95% experienced a Catumaxomab-associated adverse event, and 51% experienced postoperative complications that prevented further Catumaxomab treatments in 22% of patients. While Catumaxomab was not necessarily implicated in these postoperative complications, the authors speculated that the cytokine release associated with its use may have contributed to an inflammatory environment and increased the risk of surgical complications.
[w5fb5ac-35] Catumaxomab remains the only trifunctional monoclonal antibody approved for the treatment of MA, and several trials have proven its efficacy in palliating symptoms and prolonging PFS and TTP. Larger trials are underway investigating its use across different settings, including intra-operatively, alongside systemic chemotherapy, and as a stand-alone therapy for refractory MA. Nevertheless, its side effects can sometimes be prohibitive and it may not be appropriate in the tenuous postoperative setting.
[w5fb5ac-36] 3.2.4 Other monoclonal antibodies Several other monoclonal antibodies have been investigated in the management of MA. HEA125xOKT3, which is directed against the EpCAM antigen and the CD3 antigen found on T lymphocytes, resulted in a complete response in 8 patients and a partial response in 2 patients when injected i.p. in 10 patients suffering from MA from refractory ovarian carcinoma. 76 I.p. nivolumab produced symptomatic improvement and no further need for paracentesis in a 71-year-old patient with pancreatic cancer and MA refractory to conventional treatments. 77 2G3, an anti-mucin antibody with known reactivity against effusions from breast and ovarian tumors, demonstrated mixed results as an 131 I-radioloabeled i.p. injection in patients with MA from ovarian or breast cancer, with just 4 of 9 patients experiencing resolution or control of ascites. 78 While several other small studies have been conducted on the use of monoclonal antibodies in MA, the results have been largely inconclusive and none have demonstrated results comparable to those seen with Catumaxomab. 79
[w5fb5ac-37] 3.3 Endostar Endostar is a modified recombinant form of endostatin, a collagen fragment that has been shown to inhibit VEGF-induced tumor angiogenesis and proliferation. 80 It was approved in China in 2005 for the treatment of non-small cell lung cancer and has shown good results in the treatment of MPE, though its investigation in MA is somewhat limited. 81
[w5fb5ac-38] Most studies assessing Endostar in MA have randomized patients to receive either i.p. Endostar plus i.p. platinum-based chemotherapy or i.p. platinum-based chemotherapy alone. A 2015 meta-analysis of 25 of these randomized trials encompassing 1,253 patients with malignant serious effusions showed that the i.p. Endostar group had a significantly better effusion response rate (76% versus 48%) and improvement rate in quality of life (69% versus 44%) compared to the i.p. chemotherapy alone group. 82 However, most of these studies were conducted in patients with MPEs only as opposed to MA, which are typically more responsive to intracavitary treatments.
[w5fb5ac-39] A more recent study in 90 patients with MA from gastric cancer showed that patients who received i.p. Endostar in addition to I.p. cisplatin had a better OS, response rate, disease control rate, and improvement rate in quality of life compared to patients who received i.p. cisplatin alone, without a major difference in the rate of ascites response. 83 While these results are promising, we lack robust randomized trials conducted in the U.S. demonstrating a definitive benefit to Endostar in MA.
[w5fb5ac-40] 3.4 Steroids Some researchers have reasoned that steroids could play a role in MA, as they have been shown to downregulate VEGF and have proven effective as i.p. injections in patients with refractory ascites from end-stage renal disease. 84 With this in mind, researchers treated 15 patients with recurrent MA with i.p. Triamcinolone Hexacetonide. 85 While 13 of 15 patients required repeat paracenteses, the interval between paracenteses increased from 9.5 days to 17.5 days and self-assessed symptoms improved. Interestingly, all 7 patients whose paracentesis interval doubled after the treatment had a narrow SAAG (< 11 g/L). Because a narrow SAAG is often caused by abnormal increases in capillary permeability and is associated with elevated VEGF expression, the authors speculated that the i.p. steroid treatment functioned through inhibition of VEGF expression. A case report was also published of a man with MA from non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma whose paracentesis interval increased from 2 weeks to 8 weeks following treatment with i.p. triamcinolone acetonide. 86 Although these appear to be the only studies investigating i.p. steroids in MA, perhaps further investigation will be conducted in the future.
[w5fb5ac-41] 3.5 Oncolytic Viral Therapy Oncolytic viruses are experimental cancer treatments that function through direct tumor cell lysis and activation of an immune response. They have been studied in solid organ tumors but their investigation in MA is confined to just a handful of studies. 87 However, it is biologically plausible that oncolytic viral therapy may be more effective in MA compared to solid tumors, as MA is easier to penetrate due to the relatively less dense and less hypoxic microenvironment. 88
[w5fb5ac-42] Following preclinical mouse studies showing that i.p. injections of oncolytic viruses inhibited MA, a human study was conducted in 2022 in which 40 patients with MA from various GI cancers were administered i.p. human type 5 recombinant adenovirus (H101), an oncolytic virus genetically modified to selectively replicate only in cells with mutated p53 genes. 89 The ascites response rate was 40% and the ascites control rate was 75%, and immunocytology showed tumor cell depletion and increased dendritic cells and CD8+ T cells. Side effects were tolerable. Though these results are somewhat positive and oncolytic viral therapy has garnered significant interest in cancer treatment more broadly, few conclusions can be drawn about its use in MA at this time.
[w5fb5ac-43] 3.6 Cell-free and Concentrated Ascites Reinfusion Therapy Cell-free and concentrated ascites reinfusion therapy (CART) is a novel treatment for MA that has only been tested in Japan. The process involves draining ascites, filtering out its cellular components while leaving behind proteins such as albumin, concentrating it to decrease total volume, and finally reinfusing it intravenously. The theoretical benefit of this procedure is the avoidance of protein depletion that can occur with paracentesis.
[w5fb5ac-44] To date, there is no randomized control trial comparing CART to paracentesis. However, a systematic review following a meta-analysis of CART therapy for MA was published in 2021 and included 15 papers encompassing a total 2567 patients and 6013 procedures. 90 On average, 4.29 L of MA was collected and 0.49 L was reinfused; mean body weight fell by 3.38 kg and abdominal circumference fell by 7.86 cm; serum albumin increased by 0.14 mg/dL. Symptoms such as abdominal distension, dyspnea, and fatigue improved in most patients, and some studies reported improvements in performance status. The mean TTP following treatment was 20.7 days. Thus, while CART was developed to negate the protein depletion seen with paracentesis, it may have symptomatic benefits as well. Although this treatment modality has yet to gain traction in the U.S., it continues to be tested in Japan.
[w5fb5ac-45] 3.7 Compound Kushen Injection Compound Kushen injection (CKI) is a traditional Chinese medicine formula derived from two Chinese herbs, Kushen and Baituling. 91 It has been approved by China’s National Medical Product Administration as a treatment for cancer since 1995 and has recently been investigated in the treatment of MA. Proposed mechanisms of action include promotion of apoptosis, inhibition of angiogenesis, and suppression of the cell cycle and DNA repair pathways.
[w5fb5ac-46] A systematic review and meta-analysis published in 2023 evaluated all trials comparing i.p chemotherapy with or without CKI in the management of MA. 91 In 17 studies encompassing 1,200 patients, the objective response rate was significantly improved in patients receiving i.p. CKI plus chemotherapy compared to i.p. chemotherapy alone (80.8% vs 61.7%). Despite these results, the authors noted that many of the included studies were small, had heterogenous populations, did not follow patients for extended periods of time, and had highly variable regimens of chemotherapy and CKI. The authors warned that larger randomized trials must be conducted before recommending the product in MA.
[w5fb5ac-47] Like many of the experimental regimens previously described, CKI has garnered significant interest in various Asian countries but has yet to be thoroughly tested in the U.S. As a result, generalizing the results remains challenging, and it should be considered an interesting novel treatment that still requires much more research.
[w5fb5ac-48] 3.8 Octreotide Octreotide is a synthetic medication that mimics the hormone somatostatin and is used in several conditions including bleeding varices, hormone-producing tumors, and diseases of gastrointestinal hypersecretion. Known to inhibit VEGF, its use in MA was first described in case reports published in the 1990’s that showed modest palliative relief in some patients with symptomatic MA who received systemic Octreotide. 92 , 93 Researchers followed these case reports with a pilot study of 33 patients with symptomatic MA who were randomized to receive either intramuscular octreotide or intramuscular sodium chloride. 94 The octreotide group had a longer median TTP (28 days versus 14 days), though this was lost after adjusting for abdominal girth and volume of extracted fluid. Similarly, although the octreotide group reported significantly improved abdominal bloating, abdominal discomfort, and shortness of breath relative to the control group, overall quality of life was not significantly different. Based on these findings, the authors were unable to conclude whether octreotide provided a palliative benefit. A clinical trial is currently underway to further study octreotide in MA. 95
[w5fb5ac-49] 3.9 Automated Pump Device The alfapump device is a low-flow automated pump device produced in Switzerland. Implanted subcutaneously and battery-powered, the pump is connected to both the peritoneal cavity and the bladder through catheters, allowing for steady excretion of ascites through urination. 96
[w5fb5ac-50] First tested in small groups of patients with non-malignant ascites, in 2018 researchers described the use of the alfapump in MA. 97 In the case report, a woman with recurrent MA from breast cancer underwent alfapump implantation under general anesthesia. Her daily ascites pump volume was set at 800mL per day and she did not require further paracentesis during a 14-month follow-up. Unfortunately, there is no further discussion of complications from the pump, or whether other interventions were required. In 2019, a study was published describing the alfapump in 17 patients with MA from various primary tumors. 98 Median pump survival was 89 days, with the pump in-situ in 16 of 17 patients at the time of death or the end of the study period. Furthermore, the median number of paracenteses in a 30-day period decreased from 1.3 pre-implant to 0.08 post-implant. Unfortunately, 12 adverse events occurred in 5 patients, 7 of which were pump-related and included infection, peritonitis, wound dehiscence, and renal failure. Four other patients experienced technical issues with the pump, including pump occlusion, catheter occlusion, and pump failure due to humidity.
[w5fb5ac-51] Although the alfapump offer several benefits to ascites management, including the ability to set a “low-flow” rate that allows for continuous drainage and avoids the electrolyte and hemodynamic disturbances seen in paracentesis, it has a high rate of complications. Furthermore, patient must be maintained on long-term antibiotic prophylaxis, recharging the battery requires transcutaneous induction twice per day, and the procedure itself requires an extended hospital stay. 96 Nonetheless, it is a novel treatment for MA that perhaps will be improved upon in the coming years.
[w5fb5ac-52] 4. Mucinous Ascites Mucinous ascites is a defining feature of pseudomyxoma peritonei, a rare syndrome characterized by the diffuse accumulation of mucin-producing cancer cells within the peritoneum. Because the accumulated mucin in pseudomyxoma peritonei is characteristically gelatinous and viscous, it is particularly difficult to treat and is often refractory to many of the methods used in non-mucinous MA. Nonetheless, several “mucolytic” therapies have been investigated.
[w5fb5ac-53] A combination of bromelain and acetylcysteine (BromAc) has been described for the treatment of mucinous ascites. Bromelain is a proteinase extract from pineapple used as a digestive aid and as an analgesic and anti-inflammatory, while acetylcysteine is used to thin mucin in airway conditions. In 2021 the first phase I clinical trial was conducted in which 20 patients with mucinous tumors were treated with intratumoral or i.p. BromAc. 99 The researchers found that 73.2% of sites and 85% of patients had an objective response following a mean of 4.2 doses. Although 85% of patients experienced a total of 88 adverse events, just 11 of these were considered serious.
[w5fb5ac-54] A combination of celecoxib and myrtol standardized has also been investigated in pseudomyxoma peritonei. Celecoxib is a selective cyclooxygenase (COX)-2 inhibitor used in joint pain and arthritis but with potential apoptotic activity in colorectal adenomas, while myrtol standardized is an herbal product with mucolytic and anti-inflammatory properties used in airway infections. In 2022 our group described the use of this combination therapy taken orally in 13 patients with peritoneal spread from appendiceal mucinous neoplasms. 100 Although there was insufficient evidence to suggest a survival benefit or a decrease in the burden of mucinous ascites, most patients experienced a reduction in CEA levels in the 3 months after treatment compared to the 3 months before, and none suffered adverse events attributable from the therapy.
[w5fb5ac-55] Several other agents have been investigated. Peritoneal irrigation with both sodium bicarbonate and dextrose at the time of surgery have been tested in case reports under the supposition that they could soften and dissolve mucin, but the benefits of this approach are unclear. Other agents under investigation include streptokinase, hydrogen peroxide, ascorbic acid, and N-acetyl cysteine.
[w5fb5ac-56] 5. Conclusion Malignant ascites is a debilitating condition that portends a poor prognosis and severely impacts quality of life. While various therapies, including diuretics, serial paracenteses, permanent catheters, peritoneovenous shunting, and i.p. chemotherapy can palliate some of the worst symptoms of MA, their effectiveness is often limited or temporary. Several novel therapies are currently being investigated in the management of MA to combat these shortfalls. While some have shown tremendous promise, there remains a dearth of high-quality data demonstrating superiority over current treatments. Continued research into these therapies, conducted with larger sample sizes across diverse populations, will hopefully provide better solutions for patients suffering from this challenging condition.

[wfb2e30-3] 27 4 2012 27 4 2012 4 4 210069 87 95 2 6 2011 31 12 2011 10 1 2012 27 04 2012 15 05 2012 17 05 2012 ©2012 Baishideng. All rights reserved. 2012 Malignant ascites indicates the presence of malignant cells in the peritoneal cavity and is a grave prognostic sign. While survival in this patient population is poor, averaging about 20 wk from time of diagnosis, quality of life can be improved through palliative procedures. Selecting the appropriate treatment modality remains a careful process, which should take into account potential risks and benefits and the life expectancy of the patient. Traditional therapies, including paracentesis, peritoneovenous shunt placement and diuretics, are successful and effective in varying degrees. After careful review of the patient’s primary tumor origin, tumor biology, tumor stage, patient performance status and comorbidities, surgical debulking and intraperitoneal chemotherapy should be considered if the benefit of therapy outweighs the risk of operation because survival curves can be extended and palliation of symptomatic malignant ascites can be achieved in select patients. In patients with peritoneal carcinomatosis who do not qualify for surgical cytoreduction but suffer from the effects of malignant ascites, intraperitoneal chemotherapy can be safely and effectively administered via laparoscopic techniques. Short operative times, short hospital stays, low complication rates and ultimately symptomatic relief are the advantages of laparoscopically administering heated intraperitoneal chemotherapy, making it not only a valuable treatment modality but also the most successful treatment modality for achieving palliative cure of malignant ascites.

[wd9ed7b-1] Ann Saudi Med 1108 annsaudimed 8507355 ASM Annals of Saudi Medicine 0256-4947 0975-4466 King Faisal Specialist Hospital and Research Centre PMC3290049 PMC3290049.1 3290049 3290049 19700895 10.4103/0256-4947.55167 ASM-29-369 1 Review Management of ascites due to gastrointestinal malignancy Saif Muhammad W. Siddiqui Imran A. P. Sohail Muhammad A. From the Department of Medical Oncology, Yale University School of Medicine, New Haven, Connecticut, USA Correspondence: Muhammad W. Saif, MD · Department of Medical Oncology, Yale University School of Medicine, 333 Cedar Street, FMP: 116, New Haven, Connecticut 06520, USA · T: +1-203-737-1569 F: +1-203-785-3788 · wasif.saif@yale.edu · Accepted for publication March 2009 Sep-Oct 2009 29 5 186664 369 377 01 09 2009 30 03 2012 28 03 2013 © Annals of Saudi Medicine 2009 https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/2.0/ This is an open-access article distributed under the terms of the Creative Commons Attribution License, which permits unrestricted use, distribution, and reproduction in any medium, provided the original work is properly cited. Ascites is the pathological accumulation of fluid within the abdominal cavity. The most common cancers associated with ascites are adenocarcinomas of the ovary, breast, colon, stomach and pancreas. Symptoms include abdominal distension, nausea, vomiting, early satiety, dyspnea, lower extremity edema, weight gain and reduced mobility. There are many potential causes of ascites in cancer patients, including peritoneal carcinomatosis, malignant obstruction of draining lymphatics, portal vein thrombosis, elevated portal venous pressure from cirrhosis, congestive heart failure, constrictive pericarditis, nephrotic syndrome and peritoneal infections. Depending on the clinical presentation and expected survival, a diagnostic evaluation is usually indicated as it will impact both prognosis and the treatment approach. Key tests include serum albumin and protein and a simultaneous diagnostic paracentesis, checking ascitic fluid, WBCs, albumin, protein and cytology. Median survival after diagnosis of malignant ascites is in the range of 1 to 4 months; survival is apt to be longer for ovarian and breast cancers if systemic anti-cancer treatments are available. pmc-status-qastatus 0 pmc-status-live yes pmc-status-embargo no pmc-status-released yes pmc-prop-open-access yes pmc-prop-olf no pmc-prop-manuscript no pmc-prop-legally-suppressed no pmc-prop-has-pdf yes pmc-prop-has-supplement no pmc-prop-pdf-only no pmc-prop-suppress-copyright no pmc-prop-is-real-version no pmc-prop-is-scanned-article no pmc-prop-preprint no pmc-prop-in-epmc yes pmc-license-ref CC BY The word ascites is of Greek origin (askos) and means bag or sac. Ascites is defined as the pathological accumulation of excessive fluid within the peritoneal cavity. 1 Ascitic fluid can put pressure on the diaphragm and cause difficulty in breathing. Healthy men have little or no intraperitoneal fluid, but women may normally have as much as 20 mL depending on the phase of the menstrual cycle. Malignant ascites, the subject of this review, is a manifestation of end-stage events in a variety of cancers and is associated with significant morbidity. Malignant ascites accounts for about 10% of all cases of ascites and is usually caused by ovarian, endometrial, breast, esophageal, gastric, colorectal, lung, pancreatic, hepatobilliary and primary peritoneal carcinomas. 2 – 4 Sometimes ascites is the sole manifestation of internal malignancies.
[wd9ed7b-2] Pathophysiology The most common causes of ascites are related to portal hypertension, which is usually related to liver cirrhosis. Although lymphatic obstruction is considered the major pathophysiologic mechanism behind the formation of ascites, recent evidence suggests that immunomodulators, vascular permeability factors and metalloproteinase contribute significantly to the process ( Figure 1 ). 1 The most acceptable theory for ascites formation is peripheral arterial vasodilatation leading to underfilling of circulatory volume. Figure 1 Pathophysiology of ascites.
[wd9ed7b-3] Clinical manifestations The usual clinical presentation is a protuberant abdomen with discomfort, difficulty in breathing, fever and pain. Sometimes the hidden GI malignancy presents with ascites only. It is known that about 50% of patients with malignant ascites present with ascites at the initial diagnosis of their cancer. 5 6 The onset and progression of malignant ascites is associated with deterioration in quality of life (QoL) and a poor prognosis. According to the International Ascites Club, severity is classified as grade 1, 2 or 3 ( Table 1 ). 7 – 9 Based on associated complications like spontaneous bacterial pneumonitis (SBP) or hepatorenal syndrome (HRS) and therapeutic response, it can also be classified, as uncomplicated, complicated and refractory ascites.
[wd9ed7b-4] Table 1 Grades of ascites. Severity Grade 1 (mild) Not clinically evident, diagnosed on ultrasound Grade 2 (moderate) Proportionate sensible abdominal distension Grade 3 (severe) Noticeable tense distension of abdomen Uncomplicated 8 Not infected or associated with HRS Refractory 9 Cannot be mobilized, early recurrence after LVP, not prevented satisfactorily with medical treatment (after 1 week) Diuretic-resistant No response to intensive diuretic treatment Diuretic-intractable Drug-induced adverse effects preclude diuretic treatment
[wd9ed7b-5] Diagnosis Lab findings Routine blood work may be inconclusive, while some tests suggest specific etiologies. A prothrombin time (PT), activated partial thrombin time (APTT) and international normalized ratio (INR) must be done prior to paracentesis in all patients. Although serum tumors markers have low diagnostic specificity, they can be used for early detection. CEA antigen is used to detect relapses of colorectal cancer, but is also expressed in pancreatic, lung and breast cancers. 9 Similarly, levels of cancer antigen 125 may be elevated in ovarian, pancreatic, lung or breast cancer. 9 Ascitic fluid analysis is essential for the diagnosis of malignant ascites ( Table 2 ).
[wd9ed7b-6] Table 2 Analysis of ascitic fluid. Routine tests Optional tests Unusual tests Cell count and differential Glucose concentration Tuberculosis smear and culture Albumin concentration LDH concentration Cytology Total protein concentration Gram stain Triglyceride concentration Culture in blood culture bottles Amylase concentration Bilirubin concentration
[wd9ed7b-7] Fluid analysis Ascitic fluid analysis is essential for the diagnosis of malignant ascites. Exudative or transudative ascities on the basis of total protein content (≥2.5 or <2.5 g/dL, respectively, 10 is hampered by a large overlap between malignant and non-malignant ascites. Up to 25% of patients with cirrhosis (mostly those with cardiac cirrhosis) can have high protein levels in ascites, and 18% of malignant ascites can be low in protein levels by nature. 11
[wd9ed7b-8] The serum-to-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG) accurately identifies the presence of portal hypertension and is more useful than the protein-based exudate/transudate concept. 12 The SAAG is easily calculated by subtracting the ascitic fluid albumin value from the serum albumin value, which is obtained on the same day. The presence of a gradient of >1.1 g/dL (>11 g/L) indicates that the patient has portal hypertension with 97 percent accuracy. 12 A gradient <1.1 g/dL (<11 g/L) indicates that the patient does not have portal hypertension. 12 The SAAG need not be repeated after the initial measurement ( Table 3 ). Table 3 Classification of ascites by serum albumin ascites gradient.
[wd9ed7b-9] High albumin gradient (SAAG >1.1) Low albumin gradient (SAAG <1.1) Cirrhosis Peritoneal carcinomatosis Alcoholic hepatitis Peritoneal tuberculosis Congestive heart failure Pancreatitis Massive hepatic metastasis Serositis Constrictive pericarditis and Budd-Chiari syndrome Nephrotic syndrome
[wd9ed7b-10] Excellent discrimination between ascites due to liver disease or malignancy is reportedly provided by ascitic fluid fibronectin (sensitivity, 100%; specificity, 100%) and cholesterol levels, 12 13 although the origin of fibronectin is unclear. The gold standard for the diagnosis of malignant ascites is the presence of tumor cells in the ascetic fluid. Immunohistochemical staining combined with conventional cytologic examination increases the diagnostic sensitivity. 12
[wd9ed7b-11] Management Management of patients with ascities in GI malignancies is controversial. There are different approaches to the treatment of malignant ascites, ranging from symptomatic relief with simple drainage procedures to chemotherapy and debulking surgery aimed at treating the underlying cancer. QoL and possibly the survival of patients with malignant ascites may be improved with the increasing availability and use of appropriate and potent combination chemotherapy. 14 The onset and progression of malignant ascites is associated with deterioration in QoL and a poor prognosis. 14 There are, however, no generally accepted evidence-based guidelines for evaluation and treatment of this condition. There are also no clinical predictors that identify cancer patients who will develop this distressing entity; hence, there are no preventive measures for its development. Malignant ascites presents with a multitude of symptoms including abdominal distension, respiratory embarrassment and early satiety, swelling of limbs, impaired mobility, nutritional deficiencies and its effects, the management of which requires prompt yet effective relief of symptoms with an eye on reducing recurrence. A logical approach is to individualize treatment. The rationale in the management of malignant ascites involves consideration of survival and QoL issues. Palliative techniques play an important role in the reduction of symptoms, which bear a direct correlation to patient satisfaction and therapeutic choices. 14 15
[wd9ed7b-12] In a random sample of 80 physicians practicing in Canada, physicians were questioned on their use of different modalities in management of malignant ascites and preferences based on attitudes toward efficacy of various treatments. 14 The most commonly used means of managing malignant ascites was paracentesis, which was also felt to be the most effective by the group surveyed. After paracentesis, diuretics and peritoneovenous shunting were used most frequently, but there was no apparent consensus as to their effectiveness. 14 A survey by Lee and colleagues showed that paracentesis and diuretics were the most commonly used procedures in management of malignant ascites followed by peritoneovenous shunts, diet measures and other modalities like systemic or intraperitoneal chemotherapy. 15 Symptom-based questionnaires have helped in evaluating the symptomatology and effectiveness of abdominal paracentesis. 15 Commonly used ones include the Edmonton Symptom Assessment System-Ascites Modification (ESAS:AM), the Memorial Symptom Assessment Scale-Short Form, the European Organization for the Research and Treatment of Cancer (EORTC) Core Quality of Life Questionnaire (QLQ-C30), and the EORTC Core Quality of Life Questionnaire, 26-item pancreatic cancer module (QLQ-PAN26). 15 Most patients (78%) report that their symptoms improve after paracentesis ( Table 4 ).
[wd9ed7b-13] Table 4 Improvement following paracentesis. Symptom Improvement score (%) Abdominal bloating 42–54 Anorexia 20–37 Dyspnea 33–43 Insomnia 29–31 Fatigue 14–17 Mobility 25 Subscales that included the most distressing symptoms were most responsive. 15 The amount of fluid removed (median, 3.5 L; range, 0.3 L to 9.7 L) did not correlate with symptom improvement (r=.29, P =−.10). All questionnaires showed strong sensitivity, validity and reliability. It has been suggested that for future clinical trials of symptomatic ascites, the QLQC30 and the ESAS:AM together, or the QLQ-C30 with the addition of the QLQ-PAN26 ascites and abdominal pain subscales could be used. 16
[wd9ed7b-14] Diet Low sodium diet is the first step towards the management of ascites. It is believed to reduce the associated water retention and help reduce edema. Long-term sodium restriction has been shown to reduce recurrences and prolong the symptom-free period. 17
[wd9ed7b-15] Diuretic therapy There are no randomized controlled trials assessing the efficacy of diuretic therapy in malignant ascites. Diuretic use in managing malignant ascites is inconsistent among physicians. A survey by Lee and colleagues showed that diuretics were used by 61% of physicians treating malignant ascites (27/44), but was felt to be effective by only 45% (20/44). 18 Phase II data suggest that the efficacy of diuretics in malignant ascites depends on plasma renin/aldosterone concentration. 19 In a study by Greenway and colleagues, 20 13 of 15 patients responded to spironolactone (doses varying from 150 to 450 mg) and plasma renin activity was raised in all of 5 patients in whom it was measured ( Table 5 ).

[wd9ed7b-19] The SAAG gradient could serve as a guideline to determine response to diuretic therapy. In the prospective study by Pockros and colleagues, 19 a response to diuretics was seen in patients with ascites due to massive hepatic metastases who had a serum-ascites albumin gradient >1.1 g/dL (congruent to the serum-ascites albumin gradient of patients with benign ascites due to liver cirrhosis), whereas patients with ascites caused by peritoneal carcinomatosis or chylous malignant ascites who had no portal hypertension and a serum-ascites albumin gradient <1.1 g/dL did not respond to diuretics.
[wd9ed7b-20] Paracentesis Available data show good, although temporary relief of symptoms related to the build-up of fluid in about 90% of patients managed by paracentesis. There is no consensus on fluid withdrawal speed. Several durations have been reported, varying from 30-90 min 21 to 19-24 hours. 22 Possible complications of paracentesis include secondary peritonitis, pulmonary emboli and hypotension. 23 Fischer reported about 300 cases of abdominal paracentesis for malignant ascites where 5% dextrose was infused intravenously simultaneously and no episodes of severe hypotension were recorded. 24 Endoscopic ultrasound-guided paracentesis (EUS-P) is highly sensitive and specific for diagnosing malignant ascites. 24 The finding of malignant ascites significantly alters patient management, so an active search for ascites and use of EUS-P should be incorporated into the diagnosis and staging of upper GI and pancreaticobiliary tumors. 24 The sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, and negative predictive value of EUS-P for diagnosing malignant ascites was 94%, 100%, 100%, and 89%, respectively. 25 Studies in the context of liver disease showed that up to 5 L can be removed quickly without risk of significantly affecting plasma volume or renal function. 26 – 28 Stephenson and colleagues retrospectively analyzed 30 paracenteses in 12 patients with malignant ascites after implementing a guideline allowing up to 5 L fluid to drain without clamping and giving intravenous fluids only when specifically indicated. In the analyzed 30 paracenteses, intravenous fluids or blood products were given only in 6 procedures and there was no case of symptomatic hypotension. 29
[wd9ed7b-21] McNamara did a prospective study in the context of malignant ascites, observing 48 paracenteses in 44 patients to evaluate how much fluid needs to be drained for symptom relief. The results suggest that a significant improvement of the symptoms of abdominal pressure occurs with the removal of few liters (range 0.8-15 L, mean 5.3 L, median 4.9 L). 30 There are no randomized trials comparing paracentesis with the use of diuretics in the management of malignant ascites. A phase II study suggests that installation of Iscador M (visucs albumin extract) into the peritoneal cavity may reduce the need for repeated punctures. 31 This offers significant hope for patient compliance given the nature of recurrence of ascites and the resultant frequent hospital visits.
[wd9ed7b-22] Peritoneovenous shunts There are two main types of peritoneovenous shunt (PVS) systems, the Le Veen shunt 32 and the Denver shunt. 33 The Le Veen shunt drains ascitic fluid into the superior vena cava by a one-way valve opening at a pressure of 3 cm of water. With the Denver shunt, the valves open at a positive pressure gradient of about 1 cm of water, preventing reflux. There have been no prospective randomized studies comparing the patency rates of the two systems in malignant ascites. 34 Souter and colleagues evaluated 43 patients with malignant ascites, 16 receiving a Denver Shunt, 27 receiving a Le Veen shunt. They observed that shunt occlusion was more common with the Denver Shunt, but the two groups of patients were not selected at random and therefore may not be comparable. 35 LeVeen and Denver peritoneovenous shunt complications other than patency are comparable. The Le Veen is preferred for its superior patency in cirrhotic patients with intractable ascites. Hemorrhagic ascites and ascitic fluid protein content greater than 4.5 g/L are considered contraindications for shunting because of the higher risk of shunt occlusion. 35 Loculated ascites, portal hypertension, coagulation disorders and advanced cardiac or renal failure are also contraindications. 35 Although clinical observations and findings at necropsy indicate that peritoneovenous shunting does not result in the establishment of clinically important hematogenous metastases, 36 some studies have shown that tumor cells infused into the central venous system can lead to massive early metastases. 37 Reported median survival of patients with malignant ascites varies between 52 and 266 days, 34 indicating that patient selection assumes paramount importance in deciding upon further management. In all reported studies, patients with ovarian and breast cancer who undergo peritoneovenous shunting have the best response rate (>50%) whereas the response rate in patients with gastrointestinal cancers is far worse (10% to 15%). 37 Because of poor prognosis, it is agreed by most authors that shunt insertion is contraindicated in patients with malignant ascites due to gastrointestinal cancer. 14 An insertion of a shunt is associated with potentially fatal side-effects and costs in terms of time and money, considering that patients need to be monitored closely for at least 24 hours after operation with a central venous pressure line to monitor fluid balance. Therefore a shunt should only be used when other treatment options like diuretics have failed and when the life expectancy of the patient is long enough to derive benefit. There is no consensus on the time span; some authors advocate more than one month, 38 while others suggest an expected survival of more than 3 months. 39 40 The use of shunts has to be balanced by the potential risks of this procedure.
[wd9ed7b-23] Tenckhoff catheter This catheter is surgically placed through the wall of the abdomen to provide a point for the dialysis solution to enter and leave the peritoneal cavity during peritoneal dialysis. 41 It provides good access to the peritoneal cavity for chronic peritoneal dialysis and treatment of intraperitoneal malignancy. There are various methods for placement like blind insertion, open surgical implantation and peritoneoscopic guided placement. Ultrasound guidance is used for safe insertion. Infection is a frequent complication. Contraindications are peritonitis and sepsis.
[wd9ed7b-24] Paracentesis vs PVS placement There is no difference in survival or quality of life between patients treated with repeated abdominal paracentesis and patients treated with a PVS. 32 In one study, 20 patients underwent PVS placement and 49 patients were subjected to paracentesis ( Table 6 ). PVS placement thus provides an effective treatment option for patients with refractory malignant ascites in advanced cancer, and yields a higher likelihood of discharge compared with conventional paracentesis. 42 Table 6 Differences between paracentesis and peritoneovenous placement. Paracentensis Peritoneovenous placement Abdominal girth No significant decrease Significant decrease

[wd9ed7b-26] Image-guided biopsies When used in the context of multidisciplinary team discussion, image-guided biopsy using ultrasound (US) or computed tomography (CT) guidance is of value in planning the management of women with suspected ovarian cancer and peritoneal carcinomatosis (PC) of uncertain etiology. 42 It is essential in women believed to have ovarian cancer, but with poor performance status or with advanced disease believed beyond the scope of primary cytoreductive surgery for which staging surgical pathology will not be obtained. It provides a site-specific primary tumor diagnosis in 93% of cases and should replace diagnostic laparoscopy or laparotomy for this purpose. 43 The technique is simple, safe and effective and can be combined with palliative drainage of ascites at the same procedure. 43
[wd9ed7b-27] Recommendations Although abdominal paracentesis, diuretics and peritoneovenous shunting are commonly used procedures in management of malignant ascites, the evidence for these treatment options is weak. There are no randomized controlled trials evaluating the efficacy and safety of these procedures in malignant ascites. Available data show a good but temporary effect of abdominal paracentesis on symptom relief in patients with malignant ascites. There is no consensus on fluid withdrawal speed and concurrent intravenous hydration is not sufficiently studied. Data show that peritoneovenous shunts can control malignant ascites, but have to be balanced by the potential risks of this procedure. The use of diuretics should be considered in all patients, but has to be evaluated individually. A recommendation for further research is a randomized controlled trial comparing the use of diuretics with paracentesis in the management of malignant ascites. 34 Guidelines in the management of symptomatic malignant ascites in advanced cancer. Paracentesis is indicated for those patients who have symptoms of increasing intra-abdominal pressure. Available data show good, although temporary relief of symptoms in most patients. Symptoms like discomfort, dyspnea, nausea and vomiting seem to be significantly relieved by drainage of up to 5 L of fluid. When removing up to 5 L of fluid, intravenous fluids seem to be not routinely required (grade of recommendation: D) (Grading of the evidence and the recommendations in the guideline are based on the revised grading system by the Scottish Intercollegiate Guidelines Network (SIGN). Grades of recommendations are from grade A to D. Grade A: At least one meta-analysis, systematic review, or RCT rated as 1++ and directly applicable to the target population. Grade B: A body of evidence including studies rated as 2++ directly applicable to the target population and demonstrating overall consistency of results. Grade C: A body of evidence including studies rated as 2+ directly applicable to the target population and demonstrating overall consistency of results. Grade D: Evidence level 3 or 4 or Extrapolated evidence from studies rated as 2+). 44 If patient is hypotensive or dehydrated or known to have severe renal impairment and paracentesis is still indicated, intravenous hydration should be considered. Infusion therapy is not sufficiently studied. The only investigated therapy in malignant ascites is infusion of dextrose 5%. There is no evidence of concurrent albumin infusions in patients with malignant ascites (grade of recommendation: D). To avoid repeated paracenteses, peritoneovenous shunting may be considered. Major complications (pulmonary edema, pulmonary emboli, clinically relevant disseminated intravascular coagulation and infection) have to be expected in about 6% of patients (grade of recommendation: D). There are no randomized controlled trials assessing the efficacy of diuretic therapy in malignant ascites. The available data are controversial and there are no clear predictors to identify which patients would benefit from diuretics. The use of diuretics therefore should be considered in all patients, but has to be evaluated individually. Patients with malignant ascites due to massive hepatic metastasis seem to respond more likely to diuretics than patients with malignant ascites caused by peritoneal carcinomatosis or chylous ascites (grade of recommendation: D). 32
[wd9ed7b-28] Newer pharmacological approaches in the management of malignant ascites Intraperitoneal Immunotherapy Interferon alpha One study reported that there was resolution of malignant ascites in 3 of 10 patients treated with intraperitoneal interferon alpha-2b. 45 Another study showed a 36% complete response and 9% partial response with intraperitoneal administration of interferons in patients with ovarian cancer. 46 The only significant frequently occurring side effect was pyrexia. No significant myelosupression was observed. 46 Parenteral interferon has also shown to give positive results in one small study in which intramuscular interferon was given to 5 patients with advanced ovarian carcinoma. 47 Ascitic fluid production stopped in 2 patients, while disease remained stable in more than 1 year in 2 others and was improved in the fifth patient.
[wd9ed7b-29] Tumor necrosis factor-alpha Tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-alpha has been shown to be effective in the palliative treatment of malignant ascites. 48 One study showed a good response to TNF-alpha therapy in patients with malignant ascities. Of 22 patients, 16 had complete and 6 had partial resolution of their ascites. The response rate was highest in patients with ovarian cancer in which the tumor load was distributed in fine nodules all over the peritoneal cavity rather than as palpable bulky masses characteristic of non-ovarian tumors. Some reversible adverse affects such as fever, chills, nausea, vomiting, and fatigue were reported, but these were generally well tolerated.
[wd9ed7b-30] OK-432 A large Japanese study showed favorable results in the use of intraperitoneal injections of streptococcal antigen OK-432 in patients with malignant ascites. 49 Of 200 patients with malignant ascites of gastrointestinal malignancies, 150 were randomly selected to be administered 6 intraperitoneal injections of OK-432 at intervals of 1 week, while the remaining 50 patients served as control subjects. Of the 150 treated patients, 76 had a complete response (inability to drain any fluid) and 8 had a partial response (decrease in abdominal girth of >10 cm), giving a total response of 56%. Furthermore, the OK-432 group had a better survival time (10.2 months) compared with the control group (3.1 months). Although each of the above studies had limitations, they suggest that intraperitoneal immunotherapy may have a role in the future management of malignant ascites.
[wd9ed7b-31] Anti-VEGF therapy Decreasing permeability of vessels by inhibiting the tyrosinase kinase activity of vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) receptors has recently been shown to inhibit the formation of malignant ascites in animal models. 50 In that study, the tyrosine kinase inhibitor PTK 787 was evaluated in 2 ovarian cancer cell types. Hey-A8 cells express low levels of VEGF and grow as solid tumor foci on the surface of peritoneal organs, whereas SKOV3 i.p.1 cells express high levels of VEGF and grow as peritoneal tumors and ascites. Treatment of nude mice by means of daily oral administration of 50 mg/kg of PTK 787 was not effective against Hey A-8 tumors, but significantly inhibited the growth of SKOV3 i.p.1 cells and the formation of malignant ascites. Furthermore, survival was increased in SKOV3 mice. 51 These findings suggest that blockage of the VEGF/vascular permeability factor receptor may be a useful strategy for inhibiting the formation for malignant ascites. This conclusion is further supported by a second study in mice using VEGF-neutralizing antibodies. 51 Human studies will now be required to test this potentially promising approach toward the management of malignant ascites.
[wd9ed7b-32] Metalloproteinase inhibitors Encouraging results have also been reported with the intraperitoneal instillation of the metalloproteinase Batimastat. 52 Twenty three patients with malignant ascites had Batimastat instilled into the peritoneal cavity after paracentesis. No reaccumulation of ascites occurred after that single dose in 5 of the 23 patients, and these 5 survived for up to 112 days. Seven other patients died without reaccumulation. Nausea and vomiting were noted in the first 24 hours after batimastat treatment, but overall tolerance was good, and no significant acute peritoneal reactions were reported. Opposite results, however, were obtained in an animal study in which treatment caused dramatic tumor cell consolidation and less dispersed ascites cells compared with controls, but did not reduce ascites. 53 It is thus suggested that larger, controlled trials will be necessary before metallopreoteinase inhibitors can be recommended for routine clinical use.
[wd9ed7b-33] Radioimmunotherapy Recently, monoclonal antibody therapy has been used in treating malignant ascites with some success. Five patients with colon or ovarian cancer or mesothelioma were treated with intraperitoneal monoclonal antibody radiolabelled with 131I. 53 Three of the four assessable patients had resolution of ascites for a mean of 4 months, such that no further paracentesis or diuretic therapy was required. A phase I/II study using a novel anti-mucin monoclonal antibody 2G3 labelled with 131I12 was conducted on 11 patients with chemo-resistant ascites, secondary to ovarian or breast cancer. 54 The radioimmunotherapy was given by intraperitoneal injection and in three of the four patients who received the highest doses temporary palliation of their ascites lasting 6 weeks to death at 4 months was reported.
[wd9ed7b-34] Octreotide Octreotide, a somatostatin analogue, has been used in the symptomatic management of bowel obstruction, uncontrolled diarrhea and fistulae. It decreases the secretion of fluid by the intestinal mucosa, and increases water and electrolyte reabsorption. 55 In a study of this agent given subcutaneously in doses ranging from 200 to 600 mcg/24 h to three patients with metastatic adenocarcinoma and ascites, Cairns found that two had a reduction in ascites such that further paracentesis was not required. 55 More evidence is needed for the establishment of its efficacy in malignant ascites.
[wd9ed7b-35] Conclusion Ascities is a common finding in gastrointestinal malignancies. The effective management of ascities is necessary in treating the symptoms of these patients. We most use ascitic fluid drainage as the most common intervention. Other treatment modalities are specific for the type of malignancies present in the body. Radio- and immunotherapy and other anti-tumor therapies have been used, but none are fully successful in the management of ascites in these patients.
